## Page 1


MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE STRONG
MONODROMY CONJECTURE FOR HYPERPLANE
ARRANGEMENTS
DOUGAL DAVIS AND RUIJIE YANG
Abstract. In this work, we develop a new theory of multivariate V -filtration on
D-modules along a simple normal crossing divisor and relate it with Sabbah’s multi-
filtration. We establish several new structural results and relate them with the Hodge
filtration on free-monodromic local systems from geometric representation theory. As
applications, we prove the Strong Monodromy Conjecture and its multivariate generali-
sation for hyperplane arrangements. Along the way, we confirm both the n/d-conjecture
of Budur–Mustat¸˘a–Teitler and its multivariate form due to Budur.

1. Introduction
In this paper, we develop a new approach to the multiply-indexed V -filtration of a
holonomic D-module relative to a divisor with simple normal crossings. Our approach is
inspired by (and closely related to) the wall-crossing theory for Hodge filtrations devel-
oped by Vilonen and the first-named author [DV23] in the study of real group represen-
tations. We show that our notion is equivalent to the existing theory of Sabbah [Sab87a]
and prove several new structural results that were previously inaccessible.
As an application of the general theory, we give a proof of the Strong Monodromy
Conjecture (and its multivariable generalisation) in the case of hyperplane arrangements.
Unlike most previous work on this conjecture, which has attracted significant attention
in singularity theory, our proof is conceptual and relies very little on explicit computation
or combinatorics. As such, we hope that our methods might provide the starting point
for progress on the conjecture in the general case.
1.1. The Strong Monodromy Conjecture for hyperplane arrangements. To pro-
vide some motivation for the more abstract parts of our work, let us begin at the end with
the application. The Strong Monodromy Conjecture of Igusa [Igu88] and Denef–Loeser
[DL92] is one of the deepest and most mysterious conjectures in singularity theory. The
conjecture relates the poles of p-adic zeta functions of a hypersurface singularity to roots
of the Bernstein-Sato polynomial and monodromy eigenvalues of the Milnor fibre, pre-
dicting a striking relationship between the arithmetic and geometry of such singularities.
More precisely, if f ∈Q[x1, . . . , xn] is a polynomial and p is a prime, then the p-adic zeta
function of f is defined by
(1.1)
Zf,p(s) :=
Z
Znp
|f|s
pdµ,
where | · |p is the p-adic absolute value and dµ is the Haar measure on Qn
p. The integral
(1.1) converges when Re s > 0 and continues to a meromorphic function of s ∈C,
explicitly computable in terms of an embedded resolution of the (possibly non-reduced)
hypersurface D = {f = 0} [Den87]. Taking a suitable limit as p →1, Denef and Loeser
[DL92] also defined the topological zeta function Zglobal
f,top (s): this is a rational function in s,
calculated in terms of an embedded resolution, which now makes sense also when f has
complex coefficients (see (6.13) below). On the other side, we have the Bernstein-Sato
polynomial: the unique monic polynomial bf(s), of minimal degree, such that
bf(s)f s = P(s)f s+1
for some P ∈DCn[s].
Here DCn denotes the ring of holomorphic differential operators on Cn.
Conjecture 1.1 (Strong Monodromy Conjecture). We have
(1) (p-adic version): If f ∈Q[x1, . . . , xn], then, for all but finitely many p, if s = s0
is a pole of Zf,p(s), then s = Re s0 is a root of bf(s).
(2) (Topological version): If f ∈C[x1, . . . , xn] and s = s0 is a pole of Zglobal
f,top (s), then
s = s0 is a root of bf(s).


## Page 3


MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE MONODROMY CONJECTURE
3
There is also a “weak” form of the conjecture, which states (e.g. in the topological
setting) that if s0 is a pole of Zglobal
f,top (s) then exp(2πis0) is an eigenvalue of the monodromy
operator on the nearby cycles complex of f. The strong form implies the weak form,
since [Mal83, Kas83] the set of such monodromy eigenvalues is {exp(2πis0) | bf(s0) = 0}.
These conjectures remain widely open and have attracted persistent attention from many
mathematicians over several decades, a history we do not intend to survey in any detail;
see [Vey25, Meu16] for comprehensive overviews.
Although the Weak Monodromy Conjecture is known in several settings with non-
isolated singularities (see, for example [BMT11, LVP11, BV16, ELT22, LPS23, Que24]),
substantially fewer results are known for the strong version (see [BV26] and references
therein). Our main application is a proof of the Strong Monodromy Conjecture in a
special case (see §6.2).
Theorem 1.2. The Strong Monodromy Conjecture (Conjecture 1.1) holds when f de-
fines a hyperplane arrangement, i.e. when f is a product of linear factors with arbitrary
multiplicities.
To our knowledge, Theorem 1.2 is the first proof of a case of Conjecture 1.1 (apart from
Loeser’s work [Loe87, Loe90]), which does not rely on an explicit calculation of bf(s).
Although significantly more approachable than the general case, the special case of hy-
perplane arrangements has received considerable interest in its own right and has proved
quite difficult to establish (see [Sai16a, BW17] for earlier results). One particular chal-
lenge is that the zeta functions depend only on the combinatorics of the hyperplane
arrangement [BSY11], while the Bernstein-Sato polynomial does not [Wal17]. It is per-
haps of interest that Theorem 1.2 therefore provides a combinatorially invariant subset of
the roots of bf(s). The weak form of the monodromy conjecture for hyperplane arrange-
ments was proved by Budur–Mustat¸˘a–Teitler [BMT11], who also showed that Theorem
1.2 follows from the so-called n/d Conjecture. We prove this below (see Theorem 6.8).
Theorem 1.3 (n/d Conjecture). If f ∈C[x1, . . . , xn] defines a degree d, central essential
indecomposable hyperplane arrangement in Cn, then bf(−n/d) = 0.
In particular, this implies a slight strengthening of Theorem 1.2: for a certain canonical
resolution of singularities, every potential pole for the local zeta function is in fact a root
of bf(s) (these poles can cancel across different terms, so need not actually be poles of the
zeta function itself, see [BSY11, Appendix] or Remark 6.9). See [Wal05, BSY11, Bat20b,
SZ24, XY25] for previous work on the n/d conjecture.
Let us remark briefly on some further challenges that have previously arisen in attempts
to prove the n/d Conjecture, beyond those already present for the Strong Monodromy
Conjecture. Beyond explicit calculation, there are (broadly speaking) two main methods
to access roots of the Bernstein-Sato polynomial: via jumping numbers of multiplier
ideals [ELSV04] and poles of Archimedean zeta functions lying in (−1, 0) [Ber72]. The
root −n/d in Theorem 1.3, however, need not be either a jumping number (see [BMT11,
Remark 3.4]) nor a pole of the Archimedean zeta functions (see Remark 6.9).
Our proof of Theorem 1.2 is inspired by the following general strategy suggested in
[Bud15, BvdVWZ24]. Choose a factorisation f = f1 · · · fr and consider the statement
for the family of functions f a1
1 · · · f ar

BF = {b ∈C[s1, . . . , sr] | b(s1, . . . , sr)f s1
1 · · · f sr

Versions of the (Strong) Monodromy Conjecture and n/d Conjecture have been proposed
for Bernstein-Sato ideals and multivariable zeta functions [Bud15, Conjectures 1.13 and

1.17]. The multivariate n/d Conjecture is known for complete factorisations (i.e. when
the fi are linear) [Wu22]; see also [BSZ25, Theorem 5.4]. One might hope to deduce
from this that the single-variable conjecture holds also for f by specialising si = s. The
obstruction to carrying out this argument is the unfortunate fact that the Bernstein-Sato
ideal does not necessarily behave well under specialisation: it is not known (or necessarily
expected) that setting si = s in BF recovers the ideal (bf(s)) (see [BvdVWZ24, Page 6]).
A similar issue arises for zeta functions, see e.g. [Bud15, Definition 3.21]. Instead, our
proof carries out this strategy using a multivariate version of the V -filtration; this turns
out to have better specialisation properties than the Bernstein-Sato ideal and still carries
enough information about Bernstein-Sato polynomials to prove the theorem. (We give a
few more details on the proof in §1.5 below.) Our method is also robust enough to prove
the multivariate conjectures for hyperplane arrangements in full generality (see §6.3).
Theorem 1.4. The multivariate Strong Monodromy Conjecture and n/d Conjecture hold
for arbitrary factorisations of hyperplane arrangements.
See [Mai16, Bat20a, AABg21, Wu22, Bat23, SvdV23, BSZ25] for some previous work.
1.2. The multivariate V -filtration. With this worthy motivation in mind, let us turn
now to the technicalities of the V -filtration, which is really the main focus for most of
this paper. The classical V -filtration along a smooth hypersurface [Kas83, Mal83] is a
fundamental tool in D-module theory, used among other things to construct nearby and
vanishing cycles in this setting. It has proved very useful in the study of Bernstein-Sato
roots, especially when combined with Saito’s theory of mixed Hodge modules [Sai88,
Sai90]; see, e.g. [BS05, Sai16b, MP20, SY26].
To fix ideas, let X be a complex manifold, D ⊂X a smooth divisor and M a coherent
(left) DX-module. The V -filtration of DX along D is the decreasing filtration given by
(1.2)
V nDX = {P ∈DX | Ptk ∈OXtk+n for all k ≥0}
for n ∈Z, where for simplicity we fix a local coordinate t on X such that D = {t = 0}. A
V -filtration of M along D (see Definition 2.1 for more details) is a decreasing R-indexed
filtration V •M such that
(1) V •M is a good (i.e. finitely generated) filtration with respect to V •DX, and
(2) grα
V M := V αM/V >αM is annihilated by a product of operators of the form
−∂tt + γ with Re γ = α.
The first condition implies, in particular, that V αM is constant on a partition of R into
intervals of the form (αi, αi+1], while the second implies that the jumping points αi are the
real parts of the eigenvalues of ∂tt on M. (One can also consider a C-indexed version, in
which the jumps are the eigenvalues themselves, but we found the R-indexed version here
simpler to generalise.) The V -filtration is unique if it exists, which it always does if M
is holonomic. Very roughly, one can think of V •M as measuring either the monodromy
behaviour or the order of vanishing/pole along D of the sections of M; see [CDM24] for
a nice introduction to this topic.
Let us now replace the smooth divisor in this setup with a simple normal crossings
divisor D with irreducible components D1, . . . , Dr, with Di = {ti = 0} for some local
coordinates ti. In this setting, an analogue of the V -filtration should simultaneously mea-
sure the monodromy behaviour of M around each of the divisors Di. A naive approach
would be to consider the intersection
(1.3)
V α1
D1 M ∩· · · ∩V αr
Dr M
for (α1, . . . , αr) ∈Rr,
where V •
DiM is the usual V -filtration along the smooth divisor Di. This gives an Rr-
indexed filtration that does satisfy an analogue of the eigenvalue condition (2), but is

Sabbah’s approach gives, in some sense, a good generalisation of the V -filtration to
divisors with many components; see, e.g. [Sab87b, Sab90] for some applications. However,
since it is defined “externally” in terms of the filtrations LV •M, instead of by intrinsic
conditions, it has proved quite difficult to work with in practice. For example, the usual
V -filtration has the property that every morphism of D-modules is necessarily strict with
respect to it; while this was claimed for V •
R in [Sab87a], the claim was subsequently
retracted in [Sab90, p767] and, to the best of our knowledge, was unknown prior to the
present work.
With these drawbacks in mind, we give a different definition of a multivariate V -
filtration of the D-module M along a simple normal crossings divisor D. We explain the
general idea here, and refer the reader to §2.2 below for the precise definition.
First, we introduce the notion of a wall and chamber filtration, generalising the property
that the usual V -filtration is indexed by R but jumps only at a discrete set. A wall
and chamber filtration is defined to be an Rr-indexed filtration that jumps only along
a discrete set of hyperplanes (the walls) and is constant on the components of their
complement (the chambers). For a coherent DX-module M, we define the notion of a
good wall and chamber filtration over the Zr-indexed filtration
V nDX := V n1
D1DX ∩· · · ∩V nr
Dr DX
for n = (n1, . . . , nr) ∈Zr
by imposing a natural finite generation condition.
To define a multivariate V -filtration, we impose an additional eigenvalue condition on
the jumps across the walls. If α, β ∈Rr are separated by a wall of the form L1x1 +
· · · + Lrxr = γ, we require that the quotient V αM/V βM be annihilated by a product
of operators of the form
γ′ −L1∂t1t1 −· · · −Lr∂trtr
with Re γ′ = γ. We prove that these conditions, which reduce to the definition of the
usual V -filtration when r = 1, uniquely determine the filtration.
Theorem 1.5 (Theorem 2.18). Let M be a coherent DX-module and D ⊂X a divisor
with simple normal crossings. Then any two multivariate V -filtrations on M along D
coincide.
1This is also asserted but not proved for M holonomic [Sab87a, Footnote on page 286]; see, however
[Sab24, Corollary 4.19] for a partial result and Theorems 1.7 and 1.8 below for a general proof.

In view of Theorem 1.5, we simply write V •M for the multivariate V -filtration on M
as long as it exists.
The classical proof of Theorem 1.5 for r = 1 is a simple exercise in manipulating good
filtrations and eigenvalues. However, since hyperplanes can intersect in higher dimensions,
this argument does not easily generalise to r > 1, so new ideas are needed. Our proof
hinges on the observation (Lemma 2.20), that the defining eigenvalue condition on the
quotients across the walls is equivalent to requiring that the localisation of V •M at any
maximal ideal in C[∂t1t1, . . . , ∂trtr] be constant away from a finite set of walls passing
through a single point. This characterisation is used again and again throughout the
paper.
Using this, we are able to write down direct formulas for the localisations of
V •M (Lemma 2.21) in terms of generators for M, which uniquely determine the entire
filtration (if it exists).
With the aid of Theorem 1.5, as well as Lemma 2.20 described above, we are able to
prove that the multivariate V -filtration has the same good strictness and functoriality
properties as in the single variable case. We summarise these here:
Theorem 1.6 (Proposition 2.17, Corollary 2.22 and Theorem 2.31). The multivariate
V -filtration has the following properties.
(1) If M admits a multivariate V -filtration, then any subquotient of M does as well.
(2) If η: M →N is a morphism of coherent DX-modules and the multivariate V -
filtrations V •M and V •N exist, then η is strict with respect to these filtrations:
η(V αM) = η(M) ∩V αN
for all α ∈Rr.
(3) Let X, Y , Z be complex manifolds and let π: X →Y be a proper morphism. If
M is a coherent DX×Z-module admitting a multivariate V -filtration V •M along
X × D for some simple normal crossings divisor D ⊂Z, then the direct image
π+(M, V •M) ∈Db
coh(DY ×Z, V •DY ×Z)
is a strict complex, and the induced filtrations on the cohomology sheaves are
multivariate V -filtrations along Y × D.
These are all standard when r = 1; for example, the statement (3) can be found for
example in [MS89] or [SM22, Proposition 7.5.2] (albeit stated in a slightly different form).
Of course, it is natural to ask whether our new multivariate V -filtration coincides with
Sabbah’s. We answer this in the affirmative:
Theorem 1.7 (Theorem 2.26). For a coherent DX-module M and simple normal cross-
ings divisor D, Sabbah’s V -filtration V •
RM exists for some R ⊂Rr, which is a finite
union of cosets of Zr, if and only if the multivariate V -filtration V •M exists. In this
case, Sabbah’s V -filtration is the restriction of V •M to R ⊂Rr.
In particular, Theorems 1.6 and 1.7 resolve the confusion around strictness for Sabbah’s
filtration.
We note that the proof of Theorem 1.7 is far from being a triviality: for
example, it relies in an essential way on both Theorem 1.5 and the existence of an
adapted fan [Sab87a, §2.2].
Now, in order for this theory to be interesting, one would of course like the multivariate
V -filtration to exist in a wide variety of examples. Our next result says that this is in
fact always the case for holonomic D-modules:
Theorem 1.8 (Theorem 3.6). Let M be a holonomic DX-module and D ⊂X a divisor
with simple normal crossings. Then the multivariate V -filtration of M along D exists.

MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE MONODROMY CONJECTURE
7
Together with Theorem 1.7, Theorem 1.8 also proves existence of Sabbah’s filtration
for arbitrary holonomic D-modules; the latter is established for r = 2 in the related
setting of R-specialisable D-modules in [Sab24].
Our proof of Theorem 1.8 works roughly as follows. First, we observe that when M is
a flat meromorphic connection with poles along D and good formal structure [Sab00], the
naive formula (1.3) in fact gives a multivariate V -filtration. For general holonomic M,
we use the resolution of turning points, established by Kedlaya and Mochizuki [Ked21,
Moc11], together with Theorem 1.6 to reduce to this case.
One nice feature of this
approach is that it also gives some control over the walls in terms of a resolution (see e.g.
Corollary 3.7) and over the singular supports of the V αM (see Theorem 1.9 below).
1.3. The Malgrange-Mellin transform. With this exploration of the remote reaches
of the V -filtration under our belts, let us chart a course back towards the familiar seas
of the Bernstein-Sato polynomial. The first step is to bring expressions like f s into the
picture.
Recall that if f : X →C is a holomorphic function with the graph embedding ι =
(idX, f): X →X × C, then for any coherent DX-module M on which f acts invertibly,
we have an isomorphism of DX[s]-modues
(1.4)
ι+M ∼= M[s]f s,
where s acts on ι+M via s = −∂tt for t the coordinate on C and M[s]f s = M ⊗C[s] as
OX[s]-modules with vector fields acting by the usual formula for differentiating the symbol
f s (see (2.29)). We suggest the name Malgrange-Mellin transform for the isomorphism
(1.4), as it was first observed by Malgrange [Mal83] and is indeed a holomorphic analogue
of the Mellin transform when M = OX[f −1] (see Remark 2.53). In particular, the V -
filtration V •ι+M along the smooth divisor X ×{0} gives a filtration of M[s]f s by DX[s]-
submodules. This construction is used in many places (e.g. [Kas83, Mal83]) to relate
the V -filtration to b-functions: for example, every jumping number of the V -filtration on
ι+OX is an integer shift of a root of bf(s) and, when restricting to the lowest piece of the
Hodge filtration on ι+OCn, every jumping number between (0, 1) must be a root of bf(s),
by the work of Ein-Lazarsfeld-Smith-Virolin [ELSV04] (see also [BS05]).
More generally, given a tuple f1, . . . , fr : X →C of holomorphic functions and M =
M[(f1 · · · fr)−1], we have a Malgrange-Mellin transform
ι+M ∼= M[s1, . . . , sr]f s1

The proof of Theorem 1.9 (on which Theorem 1.10 relies) has two main ingredients:
the description of the multivariate V -filtration in terms of resolutions (which we use to
prove relative holonomicity) and the following result on the behaviour of the multivariate
V -filtration under duality (which we use to prove the flatness statements).
Theorem 1.12 (Theorem 4.14). Let M be a holonomic DX-module. Then there is a

When r = 1, Theorem 4.14 refines the classical self-duality of the nearby cycles functor

1.4. Mixed Hodge modules. So far, everything we have said about the multivariate
V -filtration holds for arbitrary holonomic D-modules. Our next few results refine some of
these statements in the more structured setting of Saito’s mixed Hodge modules. This is
of intrinsic interest, and furthermore provides some essential Hodge-theoretic input into
our application to the Strong Monodromy Conjecture.
Intuitively, mixed Hodge modules are to D-modules as mixed Hodge structures are
to vector spaces. More precisely, a mixed Hodge module (in the sense of [Sai88, Sai90])
is a regular holonomic D-module M equipped with a weight filtration W•M, a Hodge
filtration F•M and a Q-structure on the associated perverse sheaf. These structures are
required to satisfy some stringent inductive conditions, which we will not attempt to
recall in this paper; see [Sch19] for an excellent introduction. The main examples are


## Page 9


MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE MONODROMY CONJECTURE
9
polarisable variations of (pure) Hodge structure, as well as mixed versions of these. The
main upshot of the general theory is that the category of mixed Hodge modules inherits
both the functoriality (i.e. six operations) of the category of holonomic D-modules and
the strictness properties of the category of mixed Hodge structures.
As has been observed previously [MP19, SY26, DY26], for many applications (including
to singularity theory), one sometimes wants to relax this structure a little, and allow
some D-modules whose associated perverse sheaves do not admit Q-structures. There
are, broadly speaking, two ways to do this. The least ambitious is to formally extend
scalars in Saito’s theory from Q to ¯Q, see [DY26, §1.4]. This gives a notion of ¯Q-mixed
Hodge module; the underlying D-module of such a thing will always be a direct summand
of the D-module underlying a Q-mixed Hodge module of a very particular type. This is
essentially the approach taken in [MP19], for example (albeit dressed in more expensive
clothing). Alternatively, one can work from the beginning in the setting of complex mixed
Hodge modules of [SS25], which builds a version of the entire theory without mentioning
Q-structures or perverse sheaves. The latter approach, taken in [SY26], has the advantage
of being slightly more flexible (as well as giving a different perspective on polarisations,
which can be useful, see e.g. [DV23, DLY24]), but the disadvantage that the mixed part
of the theory of complex mixed Hodge modules is not yet completely written down. We
will generally state our results without specifying which theory we are using, but indicate
where we must either work in a restricted context (so that the ¯Q theory applies) or assume
some expected property of the theory of complex mixed Hodge modules.
Now suppose that f1, . . . , fr are holomorphic functions on a complex manifold X and
that M is a mixed Hodge module on X such that M = M[(f1 · · · fr)−1] as a D-module.
Then we can consider the direct image ι+M as a mixed Hodge module on X × Cr; the
Hodge filtration F• induces a filtration on V αι+M, which we denote by the same letter.
In this context, we have Hodge-filtered versions of Theorems 1.9 and 1.10.

In fact, Theorem 1.15 was really the starting point for the present work: having treated

and is equivalent to Sabbah’s filtration for arbitrary holonomic D-modules. Similarly,
Theorem 1.13 follows quite easily from Theorem 1.15 using the strictness properties of
mixed Hodge modules on the right hand side; this implies Theorems 1.9 and 1.10 when M
underlies a mixed Hodge module, which we were then able to prove directly for arbitrary
holonomic D-modules.

1.5. The Strong Monodromy Conjecture again. Finally, let us return to the Strong
Monodromy Conjecture for hyperplane arrangements, and explain how the above theory
feeds into its proof. Suppose that f is the equation of a (central) hyperplane arrangement
in X = Cn and consider its factorisation f = f a1
1 · · · f ar
r
into linear factors with multi-
plicities. We write ι: X →X × Cr for the graph embedding with respect to f1, . . . , fr.
To prove the Strong Monodromy Conjecture for f, we first argue that, for any potential
pole of the local zeta functions (with respect to the iterated blow-ups along dense edges),
there is a choice of multiplicities b1, . . . , br such that that pole becomes the log canonical
threshold of g = f b1

vary the multiplicities and use Theorems 1.9 and 1.10 to translate this to a similarly
well-behaved jump in the V -filtration for the graph embedding of our original f. Since
this jump in fact occurs in the interval (0, 1), we are able to apply a version of a re-
sult of Ein-Lazarsfeld-Smith-Virolin [ELSV04] to conclude that it is also a root of the
Bernstein-Sato polynomial of the appropriate multiplicity (see also [LY26]).
1.6. Outline of the paper. In §2, we give the definition of multivariate V -filtration,
prove some basic properties and compare it with Sabbah’s filtration. Two variants are
introduced: the restricted and localised V -filtrations, which play an important role in
the interaction of multivariate V -filtration with Hodge theory in later sections. In §3, we
study the multivariate V -filtrations on holonomic D-modules and establish the existence.
In §4, further properties of the multivariate V -filtrations on graph embeddings are proved:
flatness, relative holonomicity and duality. In §5, we examine the interaction between the
multivariate V -filtration and Hodge filtration on mixed Hodge modules. In §6, we prove
the Strong Monodromy Conjecture for hyperplane arrangements and its multivariable
generalisation.
1.7. Notation and conventions. When writing anything about D-modules, one must
make a choice whether to work in the analytic or algebraic category. As a general rule,
the latter is slightly easier than the former. All of our results go through in either setting;
we have chosen to write them in the analytic context mainly to reassure the reader (and
ourselves) that there are no unforeseen issues in this setting.
Thus, for X a complex manifold, we write OX for the sheaf of holomorphic functions
on X and DX for the sheaf of holomorphic differential operators. We work with left
DX-modules throughout, assumed at least quasi-coherent (and usually coherent or even
holonomic) unless otherwise specified.
For the sake of brevity, we will often say that various statements about sheaves on
a topological space X hold “locally on X”: this means that there exists an open cover
{Ui}i∈I of X such that the statement holds for the restrictions of the sheaves in question

12
to each Ui. For example, if X is a complex manifold, then every coherent OX-module is,
locally on X, a quotient of O⊕n
X for some n.
As is usual in Hodge theory, filtrations denoted with an upper (resp. lower) index
will be decreasing (resp. increasing). We will often work with filtrations indexed by the
partially ordered set Rr, with the partial order given by
(α1, . . . , αr) ≤(β1, . . . , βr)
if and only if
α1 ≤β1, . . . , αr ≤βr.
We will adopt the convention that vectors in Rr are denoted by bold symbols, and that
their components are denoted by the corresponding non-bold symbols. So, for example,
if α ∈Rr, we write α = (α1, . . . , αr) for the components. For 1 ≤i ≤r, we write 1i for
the vector with 1 in the ith coordinate and 0 elsewhere, and 1 = (1, . . . , 1) = Pr
i=1 1i.
Unless otherwise specified, ϵ will always denote a sufficiently small positive real number
(where “sufficiently small” is allowed to depend on other quantities in a statement).

2. The multivariate V -filtration: definition and basic properties
In this section, we introduce the multivariate V -filtration of a coherent D-module on a
complex manifold X along a divisor D with simple normal crossing, and establish some
of the fundamental properties of this notion. In §2.2 we give the definition and prove that
a multivariate V -filtration is uniquely determined if it exists (Theorem 2.18) and deduce
that morphisms are strict with respect to it (Corollary 2.22). We briefly recall Sabbah’s
approach in §2.3 and prove that our notion is equivalent to his (Theorem 2.26). In §2.4,
we prove that the multivariate V -filtrations are compatible with the formation of proper
direct images (Theorem 2.31).
In §2.5, we undertake a more detailed study of the multivariate V -filtration on DX-
modules localised away from D. We introduce here a crucial technical tool, which we use
throughout the paper, the V∗-filtration. This is defined exactly as the usual multivariate
V -filtration, but with respect to the sheaf of rings DX(∗D) instead of DX. While we show
(Corollaries 2.38 and 2.46) that the V -filtration and V∗-filtration directly determine one
another, the V∗-filtration is often technically simpler to work with.
Finally, in §2.6, we introduce the Malgrange-Mellin transform and record some elemen-
tary properties of the multivariate V -filtration on a graph embedding.
2.1. Good wall and chamber filtrations. In this subsection, we define good wall and
chamber filtrations, of which the multivariate V -filtration will be an example, and record
some basic facts about them.
To motivate the definition, we first recall the definition of the usual V -filtration of a
holonomic D-module along a smooth divisor. Let X be a complex manifold, D ⊂X a
smooth divisor and M a coherent left DX-module. We fix a local equation t = 0 for
D and a vector field ∂t such that [∂t, t] = 1. (The reader may assume that t is part of

a local coordinate system on X, and that ∂t is corresponding partial derivative.) The
V -filtration of DX is the decreasing Z-indexed filtration given by
V nDX = {P ∈DX | Ptm ∈(tm+n) for all m ≥0}.

that:
(1) V •M is decreasing (i.e. V αM ⊃V βM if α ≤β), left-continuous (i.e. V α−ϵM =

V α+ϵM = V αM for α ̸∈A + Z.
(2) The filtration V •M is good over V •DX, i.e. it is exhaustive, satisfies V nDX ·
V αM ⊂V α+nM for all n ∈Z, α ∈R, each V αM is a coherent V 0DX-module,
and there exists, locally on X, a finite set of indices {βi ∈R}i∈I such that
V αM =

Intuitively, V αM can be thought of roughly as the set of sections vanishing to order
at least α −1 along D. By [Kas83], the V -filtration is unique if it exists, which it always
does when M is holonomic.
Our aim in the next subsection is to generalise Definition 2.1 to the case where D is a
divisor with simple normal crossings. In this setting, we will be interested in filtrations
indexed by Rr, where r is the number of components of D. In this subsection, we will
consider how to generalise conditions (1) and (2) in the general setting of a sheaf of
modules over a (sufficiently nice) Zr-filtered sheaf of rings. In what follows, we use the
notation and conventions defined in §1.7 for writing vectors in Rr and for the partial
order thereon.
We first generalise (1).
Definition 2.2. Let r ≥0. A set of walls in Rr is a set W of affine linear hyperplanes
in Rr, finite modulo translation by Zr, such that each hyperplane H ∈W, called a wall,
is of the form
H = {α ∈Rr | L1α1 + · · · + Lrαr = β}
for some (non-zero) L ∈Qr
≥0 and some β ∈R. We will often regard L as a linear map
Rr →R and write H = L−1(β). Whenever we write a wall in this way, we will implicitly
assume that L ∈Qr
≥0.
Note that the set W is allowed to consist of infinitely many walls (and usually will).
We allow the case r = 0 (in which case the only possible set of walls is W = ∅) as it will
sometimes be convenient as an induction base.
Definition 2.3. Let W be a set of walls in Rr. A chamber of W is a non-empty subset
σ ⊂Rr of the form
σ = {α ∈Rr | L(α) > β if L−1(β) ∈W+, L(α) ≤β if L−1(β) ∈W−}
for some partition W = W+ ∪W−.
When r = 1, a set of walls is simply a subset W of R with finite image modulo Z,
and a chamber is an open-closed interval (α, β] wth α, β consecutive elements in W. The
shaded region in the figure below depicts an example of a chamber for a set of walls with
r = 2.


## Page 14


MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE MONODROMY CONJECTURE
14
In general, it is clear that the chambers of W form a partition of Rr and that the
interiors of the chambers of W are precisely the connected components of Rr \ S
H∈W H.
Definition 2.4. If α, β ∈Rr, we say that a wall H = L−1(γ) ∈W separates α and β if
L(α) ≤γ and L(β) > γ, or vice versa.
Note that there are only finitely many walls separating any two points and that α and
β lie in the same chamber if and only if they have no separating walls.
Definition 2.5. Let X be a topological space and F a sheaf on X. For r ∈Z≥0, a
decreasing Rr-indexed filtration of F is a collection {U αF ⊂F}α∈Rr of subsheaves such
that U αF ⊂U βF if α ≥β. We say that U •F is a wall and chamber filtration if, locally
on X, there exists a set of walls W in Rr such that if α and β lie in the same chamber
of W, then U αF = U βF. In this setting, we will call such a set W a set of walls of the
filtration U •F. If σ is a chamber of W, we write U σF := U αF for some (hence any)
α ∈σ.
When r = 1, a wall and chamber filtration is nothing but an exhaustive, decreasing,
left-continuous filtration with a finite set of jumps modulo Z.
Remark 2.6. We record two basic properties of a wall and chamber filtration U•F.
(1) For any chamber σ, we have U σF ⊂U α′F for any α′ ∈σ. Indeed, one can find a
sufficiently small ϵ > 0, and a point α ∈σ, so that α ≥α′ −ϵ1 and α′, α′ −ϵ1
lie in the same chamber. So U σF = U αF ⊂U α′−ϵ1F = U α′F.
(2) If α, β are separated by a single wall L−1(γ) and L(α) ≤γ < L(β), then U βF ⊂
U αF. Indeed, by the uniqueness of the separating wall, we must have
σα ∩σβ = L−1(γ) ∩σβ ̸= ∅,
where σα, σβ are the chambers containing α, β, respectively. Choose α′ ∈σα∩σβ,
by the property above, we have U αF = U α′F ⊇U βF.

Lemma 2.10 (Artin-Rees). Let (A, U •A, F•A) be as in Assumption 2.7. Let M be a

is also good.
We first prove a few standard results, for the lack of suitable references.
Lemma 2.11. Let B be a sheaf of OX-algebras such that
• B admits an exhaustive Zk-indexed filtration G•B such that GmB·GnB ⊂Gm+nB,
and GmB is a coherent OX-module for each m ∈Zk,
• B(K) is (left) Noetherian for any compact polycylinder K.
Then the following holds.
(1) B is coherent.

every i ≥1.

Lemma 2.12. For any sufficiently small compact polycylinder K, the rings GrUGrFA(K),
A(K), RUA(K) and RU,FA(K) are left and right Noetherian. In particular, the sheaves
of rings GrUGrFA, A, RUA and RU,FA are coherent by Lemma 2.11.

GrFA, GrUGrFA(K) are all Noetherian.

A(K) is left Noetherian. Similar argument shows that A(K) is right Noetherian.

As a corollary of Lemmas 2.10 and 2.14, we deduce that the Artin-Rees lemma also
holds for wall and chamber filtrations:
Corollary 2.15. Let M be a coherent A-module equipped with a good wall and chamber

U •N := N ∩U •M
is also a good wall and chamber filtration.

Proposition 2.17. Let f : M →N be a morphism of coherent DX-modules.
(a) If f is surjective and V •M is a multivariate V -filtration on M, then V •N :=
f(V •M) is a multivariate V -filtration on N.
(b) If f is injective and V •N is a multivariate V -filtration on N, then V •M :=
f −1(V •N) is a multivariate V -filtration on M.

The main result of this subsection is that multivariate V -filtrations are unique when
they exist.
Theorem 2.18. Let M be a coherent DX-module. Then any two multivariate V -filtrations
on M coincide.
To prove Theorem 2.18, let us fix a coherent DX-module M and a multivariate V -
filtration V •M. Regard M as a module over the polynomial ring C[s] := C[s1, . . . , sr]
by setting si = −∂titi. We recall the following elementary lemma.
Lemma 2.19 ([AM69, Proposition 3.8]). Let R be a commutative ring, M an R-module
and N1, N2 ⊂M submodules. Then N1 = N2 if and only if (N1)m = (N2)m for all maximal
ideals m ⊂R.

Proof of Theorem 2.18. Let U •M and V •M be two multivariate V -filtrations. Note that,
since the statement is local on X, we may fix a set of walls W for both U •M and V •M
by taking the union of sets of walls for U •M and V •M if necessary. Since Lemma 2.21
applies to both U •M and V •M, we have (U •M)m = (V •M)m for every maximal ideal
m. Applying Lemma 2.19, we conclude that U •M = V •M as claimed.
□
Corollary 2.22. Let f : M →N be a morphism of coherent DX-modules such that M
and N admit multivariate V -filtrations V •M and V •N. Then f is strict with respect to
these filtrations.
Proof. Let P be the image of f. Then, by Proposition 2.17, f(V •M) and V •N ∩P are
both multivariate V -filtrations on P. Hence, these two filtrations coincide by Theorem
2.18, i.e. f is strict.
□

(2) The saturation of U •M is

Here is the main result of this section.
Theorem 2.26. The following are equivalent.
(a) The multivariate V -filtration V •M exists.

2.4. Proper direct images. In this subsection, we prove that multivariate V -filtrations
behave well under proper direct images.

This filtration is compatible in the obvious sense with the V -filtrations on DX×Z and

Here the derived tensor product of filtered complexes is defined as a derived tensor product
of the corresponding Rees modules. This makes sense for both Zr-indexed and Rr-indexed
filtrations.
In the analytic setting, it is unfortunately not known in general if the proper direct
image of a coherent D-module is again coherent. This is known to be the case under
the technical assumption that the input is a good coherent D-module (see, e.g. [SM22,
Definition 2.2.6]); we will not recall the definition here, but suffice it to say that the
condition is automatic in the algebraic setting for arbitrary coherent D-modules and in the
analytic setting for holonomic D-modules [SM22, Remark 2.2.7]. Under this assumption,
we can show that the multivariate V -filtration also behaves well under direct images.
Theorem 2.31. Let X, Y and Z be complex manifolds, D ⊂Z a divisor with simple
normal crossings and π: X →Y a morphism. Suppose that M is a good coherent DX×Z-
module with a multivariate V -filtration V •M along X × D, and π × idZ is proper on
the support of M. Then the Rr-filtered complex π+(M, V •M) is strict, and the induced
filtration V •Hi(π+M) on Hi(π+M) is a multivariate V -filtration along Y × D for all n.
Moreover, any set of walls for V •M is also a set of walls for V •Hi(π+M).
Proof. We will show that the filtered complex π+(M, V •M) satisfies the conditions of
Lemma 2.30. It is clear from the construction that the cohomology sheaves Hi(RV ,Rπ+M)
are wall and chamber objects such that any set of walls for V •M is a set of walls for
Hi(RV ,Rπ+M). Note that, by definition, RV ,Rπ+M = π+RV ,RM = π+(M, V •M). Since
the DX×Z-module M is good, we may argue as in the proof of [SM22, Theorem 7.5.2
(1)] to conclude by Grauert’s theorem that π+RV ,RM has bounded cohomology and
that for all α ∈Rr, each Hi(π+RV ,α+ZrM) is a coherent graded RV DY ×Z-module. So
π+(M, V •M) ∈Db
R,coh(DY ×Z, V •DY ×Z). Finally, since the direct image is defined by
operations linear over C[s], it follows immediately from Definition 2.16 (2) that the as-
sumption on the walls in Lemma 2.30 is also satisfied. The conclusion of the theorem
now follows.
□
2.5. Restricted and localised V -filtrations. In preparation for our study of multivari-

In the classical theory of the V -filtration, one has the formula
M(∗D) = DX ⊗V 0DX V 0M(∗D)
for the localisation of a D-module along a smooth divisor in terms of its V -filtration.

generalises this to the multivariate setting.

section with a discussion of the behaviour of multivariate V -filtrations under graph em-

We also introduce the Malgrange-Mellin transform, which plays a big role

3. The multivariate V -filtration on holonomic D-modules
In this section, we study the multivariate V -filtrations on holonomic D-modules in more
detail. In §3.1, we write down the multivariate V -filtration in the case of a meromorphic
flat connection with good formal structure.
We use this in §3.2 to prove that every
holonomic D-module admits a multivariate V -filtration by a resolution of singularities
argument, and relate the walls to the numerical data specified by the resolution. We
conclude the section with a discussion of categories of fully A-specialisable D-modules,
whose V -filtrations are always defined over a restricted set E ⊂C, and show that the
multivariate V -filtrations of these are also defined over A.
3.1. The normal crossings case. In this subsection, we write down the multivariate V -
filtration explicitly for the basic local models for holonomic D-modules: flat meromorphic
connections with unramified good normal crossings singularities. We note that in the case
of regular singularities, these are nothing but flat meromorphic connections with poles
along a divisor with normal crossings. In the irregular case, further conditions need to
be imposed on the singularity of the connection to obtain simple local models.
Let X be a complex manifold, D ⊂X a divisor with simple normal crossings and M a
flat meromorphic connection on X with poles along D. In other words, M is a DX(∗D)-
module that is coherent (hence locally free) over OX(∗D). Following terminology used
by Sabbah and Kedlaya [Ked11, Definition 3.4.6] (see also [Sab00, Definition I.2.1.5], and
[Moc11, Definitions 2.2.3 and 2.3.5]), we say that M has unramified good formal structure
if, for every x ∈X, the completion
c

admits a b

(1) If j ∈J then either ϕj ∈b

For our purposes, only condition (1) will play a role. We say that M has good formal
structure if, locally on X, there exists a finite covering π: ˜X →X, ramified along D,
such that π∗M has unramified good formal structure. Here π∗is the usual pullback of a
meromorphic connection.


## Page 45


MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE MONODROMY CONJECTURE
45
In general, if D is any divisor on X and M is a flat meromorphic connection on
X with poles along D, we will sometimes say that M has (unramified) good normal
crossings singularities if D has simple normal crossings and M has (unramified) good
formal structure. By Hironaka, one can always resolve the divisor D by one with simple
normal crossings; a key theorem of Kedlaya (in the analytic case) and Mochizuki (in the
algebraic case) states similarly that any flat meromorphic connection can be resolved by
one with good normal crossings singularities:
Theorem 3.1 ([Ked21, Theorems 4.4.7 and 4.5.1] [Moc11, Theorem 1.3.3]). Let M be
a flat meromorphic connection on X with poles along an arbitrary divisor D ⊂X. Then
there exists a projective birational morphism π: ˜X →X, an isomorphism outside D,
such that ˜X is smooth, π−1(D) has simple normal crossings and π∗M has good formal
structure.
A resolution as in Theorem 3.1 is called a resolution of turning points (a turning point is
a point at which M does not have good formal structure). Roughly speaking, resolutions
of turning points play the role of resolutions of singularities in the theory of irregular
D-modules.
Example 3.2. Let X = C2 with t1, t2 the usual coordinates and consider the rank 1
connection M = (OX(∗D), d + d(t2/t1)); as a DX(∗D)-module, this is the exponential
twist OX(∗D) exp(t2/t1). Since t2/t1 does not have the form (3.1) in the definition of good
formal structure above, we see that M has a turning point at (0, 0). Letting π: ˜X →X
be the blowup at (0, 0), we have π∗M = OX(∗D) exp(π∗(t2/t1)); on the blowup, the
function π∗(t2/t1) does have the form (3.1) at every point, so π is a resolution of turning
points. Later, this example will demonstrate that resolving turning points is necessary
for constructing the multivariate V -filtration (see Example 3.9).
To prove existence of the multivariate V -filtration, we will also need the notion of
the Deligne-Malgrange lattice. For simplicity, we restrict our discussion to the case of
unramified good normal crossings singularities, although the notion can be defined more
generally (see, e.g. [Moc11, §2.7.2.1]).
Definition 3.3. Let M be a flat meromorphic connection on X, with poles along a simple
normal crossings divisor D and unramified good formal structure. A Deligne-Malgrange
lattice is a coherent OX-submodule M ⊂M, with induced connection ∇: M →M(∗D)⊗
Ω1
X, such that, M⊗OX OX(∗D) = M and, for every point x ∈X, we have a decomposition
(3.2)

(formal) vector bundles with flat connections with logarithmic singularities along D such
that, for each irreducible component Di of D containing x, every eigenvalue of the residue
ResDi ∇j has real part in [0, 1).
Note that when M has regular singularities, Deligne’s canonical extension gives a
Deligne-Malgrange lattice. In general, we have the following result.
Theorem 3.4 (e.g. [Ked11, Theorem 5.3.4] and Malgrange [Mal96]). Let M be a flat
meromorphic connection with unramified good normal crossings singularities. Then there
exists a unique Deligne-Malgrange lattice M ⊂M.

3.3. Full A-specialisability. We conclude this section with a discussion of multivariate
V -filtrations defined over a Q-linear subspace A ⊂C containing Q.
As stated, Theorem 3.6 ensures the existence of a multivariate V -filtration in which
eigenvalues are allowed to be arbitrary complex numbers. In the theory of the classical
V -filtration, however, one often finds in practice that eigenvalues can be assumed to lie
in a much smaller set, such as Q or R. In this subsection, we use this idea to define
a precise class of holonomic D-modules, called fully A-specialisable, and show that the
multivariate V -filtrations of such D-modules are always defined over A, in the sense of
Definition 2.16.
The inductive definition below is loosely inspired by the definition of mixed Hodge
modules or twistor D-modules [Sai88, SS25, Sab05, Moc11]. Roughly speaking, the fully
A-specialisable D-modules form the largest class of holonomic D-modules that is closed
under standard functors and for which (single-variable) V -filtrations are always defined
over A.
Definition 3.10. Let M be a holonomic DX-module. We define the condition that M
be fully A-specialisable inductively on the dimension as follows.
(1) If dim supp M = 0, then M is fully A-specialisable.
(2) If dim supp M ≥1 and M is simple, then M is fully A-specialisable if for every
open subset U ⊂X and every holomorphic function f on U such that supp M ̸⊂
f −1(0), the V -filtration ι+(M|U) is defined over A and grα
V ι+(M|U) is fully A-
specialisable for all α ∈R. Here ι: U →U × C is the graph embedding of f.
If M is not simple, then M is defined to be fully A-specialisable if each of its
composition factors is.
Note that the simplicity assumption in (2) is imposed for convenience of the inductive
definition to ensure that dim supp Grα
V M < dim supp M for all α. If M• is a complex
of D-modules with holonomic cohomologies, we say it is fully A-specialisable if each
cohomology Hj(M•) is fully A-specialisable.
Some basic properties immediately follow from the properties of the (single-variable)
V -filtration and how they interact with standard functors. In below, any D-module is
assumed to be holonomic.
(1) If M is fully A-specialisable, then the V -filtration along any smooth divisor is
defined over A and Grα
V M is fully A-specialisable for all α ∈R.
(2) The fully A-specialisability is closed under subquotients and extensions.
Fur-
thermore, if M•

Proposition 3.12. If a holonomic DX-module M is fully A-specialisable, then its mul-
tivariate V -filtration along any simple normal crossing divisor D is defined over A.
Proof. By the basic properties of the class of fully A-specialisable D-modules above, the
proof of Theorem 3.6 reduces the proposition to the case where M is a flat meromorphic
connection with good formal structure. In this case, the proposition follows from Propo-
sition 3.5, since the V -filtration of M along every irreducible component of D is defined
over A.
□
Remark 3.13. In order for Proposition 3.12 to be useful in practice, one needs a supply of
examples of fully A-specialisable D-modules. In this paper, we will obtain such examples
(for A = Q or R) from the D-modules underlying mixed Hodge modules (see Corollary
5.8). More generally, one can show that the D-module associated to a flat meromorphic
connection with good formal structure is fully A-specialisable if and only if the V -filtration
along each irreducible component of D is defined over A. Applying standard functors,
this gives a wide class of fully A-specialisable D-modules: for example, starting from
the fact that the V -filtration of the trivial connection along a smooth divisor is always
defined over Q, one sees in this way that all holonomic D-modules of geometric origin
are fully Q-specialisable.
4. The multivariate V -filtration as a DX[s]-module
In this section, we study further properties of the multivariate V -filtration V αι+M
on the graph embedding of a holonomic DX-module M with respect to a sequence of
holomorphic functions, regarded via the Malgrange-Mellin transform (Proposition 2.52)
as a module over DX[s]. In §4.1, we review the theory of relative holonomicity and show
(Proposition 4.3) that V αι+M is always a relative holonomic DX[s]-module. In §4.2, we
prove a self-duality theorem for the V∗-filtration (Theorem 4.14), generalising the usual
self-duality of the nearby cycles functor. Finally, in §4.3, we prove (Theorem 4.17) that
the DX[s]-modules V α
∗ι+M are always flat over C[s], as well as a similar statement for
the quotients V α
∗ι+M/V β
∗ι+M. These flatness statements play a key role in our study
of the Strong Monodromy Conjecture for hyperplane arrangements in §6.
Unless otherwise specified, we work throughout in the following setting: fix a complex
manifold X, holomorphic functions f1, . . . , fr : X →C, and a holonomic DX-module M.
4.1. Relative holonomicity. In this subsection, we recall the notion of a relative holo-
nomic DX-module over a smooth affine variety from [BvdVWZ21].
We review some
general properties of such modules (which we will need later on) and show that the
multivariate V -filtration on a graph embedding gives an example.

not vanish at the generic points of a given collection of subvarieties of different dimensions

5. The Hodge filtration and the V -filtration
In this section, we specialise our study of the multivariate V -filtration further from
general holonomic D-modules to the more structured setting of mixed Hodge modules.
We recall some of the general theory of mixed Hodge modules in §5.1; the main new feature
for our purposes is the addition of a very special good filtration, the Hodge filtration. In
§5.2, we show (Proposition 5.20) that the V -filtration formula for the ∗-localisation along
a simple normal crossings divisor holds also with the Hodge filtration in place. Using this,
we show in §5.3 that, conversely, the multivariate V∗-filtration on the graph embedding
of a mixed Hodge module can be recovered from the Hodge filtrations on a sequence
of auxiliary mixed Hodge modules; this is a multivariate version of our construction
in [DY26] for the single-variable V -filtration. As applications, we deduce in §5.4 that
the Hodge filtration and the multivariate V -filtration interact rather well, at least if we
restrict to the positive orthant: for example, we prove Hodge-filtered versions of the
flatness theorem (Theorem 5.33), the monomial change of functions (Theorem 5.38) and
strictness of the direct images under proper maps (Theorem 5.39).
5.1. Recollection on mixed Hodge modules. In this subsection, we recall some as-
pects of the theory of mixed Hodge modules.
Let us first fix some conventions, as the phrase “mixed Hodge module” can mean a
few different things depending on context. If X is a complex manifold, we will write
MHMQ(X) for the Q-linear abelian category of polarisable mixed Hodge modules on X
defined by Saito in [Sai90, §2.17]. We will refer to the objects simply as Q-mixed Hodge
modules. For example, if X is a point, then MHMQ(X) is the category of polarisable
Q-mixed Hodge structures [Sai90, Theorem 3.9]. For general X, an object in MHMQ(X)
is specified by a tuple
(M, F•M, W•M, K),
where M is a regular holonomic DX-module, F•M is a good filtration over the order
filtration F•DX, W•M is a finite filtration by DX-modules and K is a Q-perverse sheaf
such that K ⊗Q C ∼= DRX(M). These data are required to satisfy a complicated set of
inductive conditions amounting roughly to the requirement that any specialisation to a
point is a Q-mixed Hodge structure.
Remark 5.1. We work in this paper primarily with analytic mixed Hodge modules
on complex manifolds. There is also a more restrictive notion of algebraic mixed Hodge
modules on a complex algebraic variety X [Sai90, §4]. In the polarisable setting considered


## Page 62


MULTIVARIATE V -FILTRATIONS AND THE MONODROMY CONJECTURE
62
here, one can define the category of algebraic mixed Hodge modules on X to be the full
subcategory of analytic mixed Hodge modules that can be extended to some (hence any)
proper algebraic compactification of X. In particular, all our results proved in the analytic
setting hold also in the algebraic one.
In this paper, it will be convenient also to work with larger coefficient fields than Q.
Here are two ways to achieve this. First, if K ⊂C is an algebraic extension of Q, then we
can define a K-linear category MHMK(X) by formally extending scalars in MHMQ(X)
from Q to K; see [DY26, §1.4]. In this setting, most theorems extend formally from the
case of Q-mixed Hodge modules to K-mixed Hodge modules. If K = C, on the other
hand, then one also has the C-linear category MHMC(X) of complex mixed Hodge modules,
which is the topic of the (unfinished) book project [SS25] (see also [DV25, Appendix A.1]
and [DV23, §2.1] for some general discussion). This theory, which sits somewhere between
Saito’s theory and Mochizuki’s theory of mixed twistor D-modules [Moc15], is developed
in parallel to the Q-linear theory, but with C-Hodge structures as the prototype instead
of Q-Hodge structures.
For the results we will consider here, the complex theory MHMC(X) is the most natural
context. We will therefore write simply
MHM(X) := MHMC(X)
and refer to its objects simply as mixed Hodge modules. Since there is no complete
reference at the time of writing, however, we will have to take certain expected properties
of this theory on faith; we will spell these out when they occur, and will indicate when
needed how to modify the statements and proofs of our results in the better-founded
theory over algebraic extensions of Q.
Let us first record how the theories with different coefficients are related:
Proposition 5.2. Let K = C or an algebraic extension of Q. Then there is an exact
base-extension functor
K ⊗Q −: MHMQ(X) →MHMK(X).
This is true by definition when K is algebraic over Q. When K = C we take it to be
an expected property of the theory of complex mixed Hodge modules.
Our main interest in mixed Hodge modules will be their underlying D-modules equipped
with Hodge and weight filtrations. For future reference, we record the main properties of
this structure. In the statement below, we write MFW(DX)rh for the category of tuples
(M, F•M, W•M), where M is a regular holonomic DX-module, F•M is a good filtration
and W•M is a filtration by DX-submodules.
Proposition 5.3. Let K be C or an algebraic extension of Q. Then for any complex
manifold X, there is a functor
MHMK(X) →MFW(DX)rh
M 7→(M, F•M, W•M)
such that the composition with the forgetful functor to Coh(DX) is faithful and exact.
Moreover, if M →N is a morphism in MHMK(X), then the morphism (M, F•M, W•M) →
(N, F•N, W•N) is bi-strict. The forgetful functors to MFW)(DX)rh commute with the
coefficient extensions of Proposition 5.2.
This is a standard fact in the case K = Q (cf. [Sai88, Proposition 5.1.14]), while
the extension to K algebraic over Q is explained briefly in [DY26, §1.4].
We take it
as a desideratum of the complex theory (which should follow by essentially the same
arguments as the rational case).
The category MHMK(X) also comes equipped with

D-modules by
(M(1), W•M(1), F•M(1)) = (M, W•+2M, F•−1M).
This is true by construction in all settings.
One of the main points of constructing the category MHMK(X) is that it has some
particularly good functoriality properties. We will recall a few of these below.
First, if f : X →Y is a proper morphism of complex manifolds, then we have Laumon’s
filtered direct image functor

Theorem 5.4. Assume that f : X →Y is a projective morphism.
Then there exist
functors
Hif+ : MHMK(X) →MHMK(Y )
for i ∈Z, such that for any M ∈MHMK(X), the filtered complex f+(M, F•) is strict

The case K = Q is [Sai90, Theorem 2.14 and 2.18], while the case where K is algebraic
over Q follows by extension of scalars. The case K = C follows by the same argument
by reduction to the pure case, which is [SS25, Theorem 14.3.1]. Note that in the setting
of algebraic mixed Hodge modules, the cohomological functors Hif+ can be upgraded to
a functor between derived categories [Sai90, Theorem 4.3], but this is not known in the
analytic setting.
As an elementary special case, when f : X →Y is a closed immersion, we have Hif+ =
0 for i ̸= 0, so we get an honest exact functor
f+ = H0f+ : MHMK(X) →MHMK(Y ).
Proposition 5.5 (Kashiwara’s equivalence for mixed Hodge modules). For a closed im-
mersion f as above, the functor f+ is fully faithful, with image equal to the full subcategory
of mixed Hodge modules on Y whose underlying DY -modules are supported in X.
The full faithfulness of f+ is a relatively easy consequence of the definitions of the
functor using the analogous statement for the underlying D-modules. The statement
about the essential image is baked into the inductive definition of mixed Hodge module.
As alluded to above, the category of mixed Hodge modules is defined so that it also
behaves well under specialisation. More precisely, it has the following good property with
respect to the V -filtration.

ules and operations to construct new mixed Hodge modules out of old ones. In order for

object in MHMQ(U)X with underlying bi-filtered DX-module

Lemma 5.35. Let M be a bounded below graded C[s]-module. Then the following state-
ments are equivalent.

ker(π) = 0 = coker(π) and so π is an isomorphism. In particular, M is a free C[s]-module,
i.e. (1) holds.

6. The Strong Monodromy Conjecture for hyperplane arrangements
In this section, as a demonstration of the general theory developed in §§2–5, we prove
the Strong Monodromy Conjecture for hyperplane arrangements and its multivariable
version due to Budur.
6.1. Multivariate V -filtration of hyperplane arrangements. In this subsection, we
establish a structural lemma about the multivariate V -filtration of the graph embedding
of hyperplane arrangements that we will use in the proof
Assume that f = Qr
i=1 f di
i
∈C[x1, . . . , xn] defines a hyperplane arrangement in X =
Cn, where fi are linear and di ∈Z>0. Set D := f −1(0) = Pr
i=1 diDi, where Di = div(fi)
are the irreducible components of D.
We recall some terminologies and basic facts following [BMT11, §2]. A hyperplane
arrangement is central if each fi is homogeneous. A linear subspace W ⊂Cn is called an
edge, if it is an intersection of some hyperplanes Di. A central hyperplane arrangement is
essential if {0} is an edge, and is indecomposable if there is no linear change of coordinates
on Cn such that f can be written as the product of two non-constant polynomials in
disjoint sets of variables. An edge W is dense if the induced hyperplane arrangement
im{∪Di⊇WDi →Cn/W} is essential and indecomposable. We assume throughout this
section that
D is central, essential, and indecomposable.
Under this assumption, let ι: X →X × Cr be the graph embedding of f1, . . . , fr, and let
M = ι+OX be the direct image D-module. Consider the multivariate V -filtration V •M,
with respect to the coordinates on Cr. Via the DX[s]-module isomorphism

from (2.31), we will identify M as a sub-DX[s]-module of OX(∗D)[s]f s. For α ∈Rr,

Lemma 6.4. For any edge W ̸= {0}, one has dim spanC{fi | fi|W ̸= 0} > dim W.

V1 = spanC{fi | fi|W = 0},

linear functions with separate variables. This contradicts the indecomposability of D, so
dim V2 > n −dim V1 = dim W as claimed.

If f defines a hyperplane arrangement, we can always make a linear change of variables
so that f(0) = 0. In this case, we have a local version of Theorem 1.2.
Theorem 6.7. If f ∈C[x1, . . . , xn] defines a hyperplane arrangement and f(0) = 0, then
any pole s0 of Zf,top(s) is a root of bf(s).

Remark 6.9. A different strategy for proving Theorem 6.8, which has been proposed else-
where (cf. [SZ24, Question 1.12]) would be to show that −n/d is a pole of the Archimedean
zeta function Zf (see, e.g. [SZ24] or [DLY24] for the definition); since n/d < 1, this would
imply bf(−n/d) = 0 by Bernstein’s functional equation for Zf. This approach is used,
for example, in [SZ24] to prove a generic version of the n/d-conjecture. We show below,
however, that −n/d need not always be a pole of Zf.
